\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n}=(2n+1)i\pi T),

(140)

where
k_{0}
is the fourth (temporal) component of momentum in Minkowski space-time and the
f(k_{0})
is a meromorphic function.
We know hyperbolic tangent has poles at
\tanh(\frac{\pi i}{2}+n\pi i)
with residue unity.
Therefore, one can insert hyperbolic tangent with suitable argument as
Kapusta
;
Le_Bellac

\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n}=(2n+1)i\pi T)\,\textrm{Res}\left[\frac{\beta}{2}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)\right],

(141)

where hyperbolic tangent corresponds to poles at

\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)=\tanh(\frac{\pi i}{2}+n\pi i)\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,k_{0}=\frac{(2n+1)i\pi n}{\beta}=i\omega_{n}

(142)

with residues
2/\beta
.
Then one can write (
141
) without any loss of generality as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0})\,\textrm{Res}\left[\frac{\beta}{2}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\frac{\beta}{2}\ {\rm{Res}}\left[f(k_{0})\,\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right);\Rightarrow{\rm{poles:}}k_{0}=i\omega_{n}=\frac{(2n+1)i\pi}{\beta}\right].

(143)

Employing the residue theorem, as before, in reverse the sum over
residues can now be expressed as an integral over contours
C_{1}
and
C_{2}

in
\angle k_{0}
enclosing the poles of the meromorphic function
f(k_{0})

but excluding the poles of hyperbolic tangent (
k_{0}=i\omega_{n}=(2n+1)\pi iT
)
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0})\,\frac{\beta}{2}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{T}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C_{1}\cup C_{2}}dk_{0}\,f(k_{0})\frac{\beta}{2}\,\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

(144)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C_{1}\cup C_{2}}dk_{0}\,f(k_{0})\frac{1}{2}\,\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right),

where contours
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are represented in Fig.
7

Figure 7:
Contours
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
that include the poles of the meromorphic function
f(k_{0})

in complex
k_{0}
plane for fermion with zero chemical potential (
\mu=0
). The contours are also shifted
by an amount
\pm\epsilon
from the
{\rm{Im}}k_{0}
line to exclude the poles of
\tanh(\beta k_{0}/2)
at

k_{0}=(2n+1)i\pi T;\,n=0,1,2,\cdots
.

4.2.1
Separation of vacuum and matter part

We know

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\exp(\beta k_{0}/2)-\exp(-\beta k_{0}/2)}{\exp(\beta k_{0}/2)+\exp(-\beta k_{0}/2)}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\exp(\beta k_{0})-1}{\exp(\beta k_{0})+1}

(145)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\frac{2}{{\exp(\beta k_{0})+1}}\right].

Using (
145
) and (
144
) in (
140
) the fermionic sum integral can be written as

\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C_{1}\cup C_{2}}\,dk_{0}\,f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})\,\left[\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{{\exp(\beta k_{0})+1}}\right]

(146)

which separates
T=0
(vacuum) and
T\neq 0
(medium) part.

4.2.2
Choice of contour

Proceeding same way as the bosonic case before, one can write

\displaystyle T\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\,d(k_{0})\,\frac{1}{2}\Big[f(k_{0})+f(-k_{0})\Big]

(147)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}\,d(k_{0})\Big[f(k_{0})+f(-k_{0})\Big]\frac{1}{{\exp(\beta k_{0})+1}}.

We note that the Contour is in right half plane. The first term in RHS is vacuum contribution whereas the second term is matter contribution.
One can use either (
144
) or (
146
) or (
147
) conveniently.

4.3
Examples of Frequency Sum for Bosonic Case

The Euclidean time bosonic Green’s function in coordinate space can be written as

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(\bm{\vec{x}},\tau)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\,-\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,\frac{e^{-i\omega_{n}\tau}}{\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}},

(148)

with
\omega_{n}=2\pi nT
and
\omega_{k}=\sqrt{k^{2}+m^{2}}
. Now we put back
i\omega_{n}=k_{0}
, the fourth component of Minkowski
momentum to write the standard form as given in (
130
) without any loss generality:

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(\bm{\vec{x}},\tau)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\,\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,\ \frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\,\,

(149)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\,\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,\ \frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n}).

Now we perform the frequency sum.
As discussed the sum integration can be performed using either (
134
) or (
136
) or (
139
) conveniently.
However, we would use (
134
) for the purpose

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}

(150)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C}dk_{0}\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\coth\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right).

We now rewrite (
150
) here

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}

(151)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C}dk_{0}\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\coth\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right).

Using (
139
), one gets (we drop
\tau>0
from all equations below)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\,\frac{1}{2}\Big[f(k_{0})+f(-k_{0})\Big]+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\Big[f(k_{0})+f(-k_{0})\Big]\frac{1}{{e^{\beta k_{0}}-1}}

(152)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\,\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}+\frac{e^{k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\left[\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}+\frac{e^{k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{{e^{\beta k_{0}}-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{1}^{0}+I_{2}^{0}+I_{3}^{\beta}+I_{4}^{\beta},

where
I_{1}^{0}
and
I_{2}^{0}
are for
T=0
parts whereas
I_{3}^{\beta}
and
I_{4}^{\beta}
are for
T\neq 0
parts. We now evaluate them below:

Figure 8:
Contour corresponding to the Integral
I_{1}^{0}
in the complex
k_{0}
plane.

\displaystyle I_{1}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}

(153)

\bullet

Poles:
k_{0}=\pm\omega_{k}

\bullet

Convergence: for
\tau>0
,
e^{-k_{0}\tau}
converges in the domain
0\leq\tau\leq\beta
only for
k_{0}=\omega_{k}
.
The relevant contour is given in Fig.
8
.

I_{1}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(2\pi i)\,\lim_{k_{0}\rightarrow\omega_{k}}\,\,\frac{(k_{0}-\omega_{k})}{(k_{0}-\omega_{k})(k_{0}+\omega_{k})}e^{-k_{0}\tau}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}.

(154)

\displaystyle I_{2}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{-i\infty}^{+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\,\frac{e^{k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}

(155)

Figure 9:
Contour corresponding to the Integral
I_{2}^{0}
in complex
k_{0}
plane.

\bullet

Poles:
k_{0}=\pm\omega_{k}

\bullet

Convergence: for
\tau>0
,
e^{k_{0}\tau}
converges in the domain
0\leq\tau\leq\beta
only for
k_{0}=-\omega_{k}
.
The relevant contour is given in Fig.
9
. Note that the contour is anticlockwise so it will induct a negative sign.

I_{2}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(-2\pi i)\,\lim_{k_{0}\rightarrow-\omega_{k}}\,\,\frac{(k_{0}+\omega_{k})}{(k_{0}-\omega_{k})(k_{0}+\omega_{k})}e^{k_{0}\tau}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}=I_{1}^{0}.

(156)

\displaystyle I_{3}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\frac{1}{{e^{\beta k_{0}}-1}}.

(157)

\bullet

Poles:
k_{0}=\pm\omega_{k}

\bullet

Convergence: for
\tau>0
,
e^{-k_{0}\tau}/(e^{\beta k_{0}}-1)\sim\ e^{-k_{0}\tau}\,e^{-\beta k_{0}}

converges in the domain
0\leq\tau\leq\beta
only for
k_{0}=\omega_{k}
.
The relevant contour is given in Fig.
10
.

Figure 10:
Contour corresponding to the Integral
I_{3}^{\beta}
in complex
k_{0}
plane.

I_{3}^{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(2\pi i)\,\lim_{k_{0}\rightarrow\omega_{k}}\,\,\frac{(k_{0}+\omega_{k})}{(k_{0}-\omega_{k})(k_{0}-\omega_{k})}e^{k_{0}\tau}=\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\,\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{{e^{\beta k_{0}}-1}}=\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}\,\,n_{B}(\omega_{k}).

(158)

\displaystyle I_{4}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}\,dk_{0}\frac{e^{k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\frac{1}{{e^{\beta k_{0}}-1}}.

(159)

Figure 11:
Contour corresponding to the Integral
I_{4}^{\beta}
in complex
k_{0}
plane.

\bullet

Poles:
k_{0}=\pm\omega_{k}

\bullet

Convergence: for
\tau>0
,
e^{k_{0}\tau}/(e^{\beta k_{0}}-1)\sim\ e^{k_{0}\tau}\,e^{-\beta k_{0}}

converges in the domain
0\leq\tau\leq\beta
only for
k_{0}=\omega_{k}
and
\beta>\tau
.
The relevant contour is given in Fig.
11

I_{4}^{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(2\pi i)\,\lim_{k_{0}\rightarrow\omega_{k}}\,\,\frac{(k_{0}+\omega_{k})}{(k_{0}-\omega_{k})(k_{0}-\omega_{k})}e^{k_{0}\tau}=\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\,\frac{e^{\omega_{k}\tau}}{{e^{\beta k_{0}}-1}}=\frac{e^{\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}\,\,n_{B}(\omega_{k}).

(160)

At this point it is worth noting that the contours for
I_{1}^{0},\,\,I_{3}^{\beta}
and
I_{4}^{\beta}
are in right half plane whereas
that for
I_{2}^{0}
is in the left half plane.

Now combining (
154
), (
156
), (
158
) and (
160
) with (
152
), one gets for
\tau>0
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{n=+\infty}\,f(k_{0}=i\omega_{n})

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\,I_{1}^{0}+I_{2}^{0}+I_{3}^{\beta}+I_{4}^{\beta}

(161)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C}dk_{0}\,\frac{e^{-k_{0}\tau}}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\coth\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(2\pi i)\,\left[\left.R_{1}\right|_{k_{0}=\omega_{k}}+\left.R_{2}\right|_{k_{0}=-\omega_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}+\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}n_{B}(\omega_{k})+\frac{e^{\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}n_{B}(\omega_{k})

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}(1+n_{B}(\omega_{k}))+\frac{e^{\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}n_{B}(\omega_{k}).

Again putting (
161
) in (
148
) on gets Euclidean time Green’s function for
\tau>0
as

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(\bm{\vec{x}},\tau)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\,\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\,\Big[(1+n_{B}(\omega_{k}))\,\,e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,+n_{B}(\omega_{k})\,\,e^{\omega_{k}\tau}\,\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,\Big].

(162)

Now changing
\bm{\vec{k}}\rightarrow-\bm{\vec{k}}
in the second term inside the square braces, leads to the Euclidean time green’s
function as

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(\bm{\vec{x}},\tau)

\displaystyle=\atop{\tau>0}

\displaystyle\,\,\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\,\Big[(1+n_{B}(\omega_{k}))\,\,e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}\,\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,+n_{B}(\omega_{k})\,\,e^{\omega_{k}\tau}\,\,e^{-i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,\Big].

(163)

If one makes a Wick rotation
\tau\rightarrow it
to Minkowski time, it becomes

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(\bm{\vec{x}},t)

\displaystyle=\atop{t>0}

\displaystyle\,\,\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\,\Big[(1+n_{B}(\omega_{k}))\,\,e^{-i\omega_{k}t}\,\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,+n_{B}(\omega_{k})\,\,e^{i\omega_{k}t}\,\,e^{-i\bm{\vec{k}}\cdot\bm{\vec{x}}}\,\Big]

(164)

\displaystyle=\atop{t>0}

\displaystyle\,\,\int\frac{{d^{3}}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\,\Big[(1+n_{B}(\omega_{k}))\,\,e^{-iK\cdot X}\,\,+n_{B}(\omega_{k})\,\,e^{iK\cdot X}\,\Big],

which agrees with the real time Green’s function in (
106
).

5
Scalar Theory

5.1
Tadpole Diagram in
\lambda\phi^{4}
Theory

The
{\cal S}
-matrix in Euclidean time as given in (
90
)

{\cal S}(\beta)={\cal T}\left[\exp\left({-\int_{0}^{\beta}}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)\right]=\sum_{N}\frac{[\cdots]^{N}}{N!},

(165)

where
{\cal T}
is the time ordered product in imaginary time
\tau=it
. Following the same procedure as
T=0
field theory
the scalar Lagrangian is given as

\displaystyle{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}\right]-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}={\cal L}_{0}\,+\,{\cal L}_{I},

(166)

where where
\phi
is a scalar field,
\lambda
is the coupling in the theory and
{\cal L}_{0}
is the free scalar field Lagrangian.
The interaction Lagrangian is

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{I}=-\,\frac{1}{4!}\lambda\phi^{4}.

(167)

4!
comes from how many ways the
\phi
fields can be arranged.
Vertex is
\,\,-i\lambda
(same as vacuum). The interaction Lagrangian in (
167
) will lead to the tadpole diagram as shown in Fig
12
.

Figure 12:
Tadpole diagram in
\lambda\phi^{4}
theory.

Symmetry factor: After contraction how the remaining legs (fields) are connected to the interaction
vertex. The topology of the tadpole diagram in
\phi^{4}
theory is given in Fig.
12
, which results from one contraction
involving two
\phi
fields and remaining two
\phi
fields can then be connected to the vertex in two ways. So, the symmetry factor is
1/2

(same as vacuum).

The amplitude corresponding to the tadpole diagram in Fig
12
can be written following the Feynman rules defined
earlier as

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,(-i\lambda)\,\int\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\frac{i}{K^{2}-m^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\,(-i\lambda)\,\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{k_{0}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{i}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}

(168)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\lambda\,\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{k_{0}}\,\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}},

where
\omega_{k}=\sqrt{k^{2}+m^{2}}
and
\Pi
is independent of external momentum.

Now the function under the frequency sum is same as those in (
149
)
for
\tau=0
. So one can write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{k_{0}}\,\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\left[I_{1}^{0}+I_{2}^{0}+I_{3}^{\beta}+I_{4}^{\beta}\right]\right|_{\tau=0}

(169)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\left[\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}+\frac{e^{-\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}n_{B}(\omega_{k})+\frac{e^{\omega_{k}\tau}}{2\omega_{k}}n_{B}(\omega_{k})\right]\right|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\omega_{k}}\Big[1+2n_{B}(\omega_{k})\Big].

Using (
169
) in (
168
), one can write

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda\,\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{\omega_{k}}\Big[1+2n_{B}(\omega_{k})\Big].

(170)

Now, we note that

\bullet

the first term is the vacuum (
T=0
) contribution and it is ultraviolate divergent. This could be regulated using
dimensional regularisation at
T=0
and it vanishes.

\bullet

the second term is finite as it involves the Bose-Einstein distribution, which falls of exponentially for
large
\omega_{k}
or momentum. The finite temperature does not cause any ultraviolate divergence but induct infrared
divergence
4
4

4

At
m=0
and
|k|\rightarrow 0\,\Rightarrow n_{B}(\omega_{k}=0)=1/(1-1)\rightarrow\infty
there is an
infrared divergence due to zero bosonic mode caused by
\omega_{n}=2\pi nT
for
n=0
. We will come back later how can this
infrared divergence be regulated.
.

The second term can be written for (
m=0
) as

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda\,\frac{4\pi}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,dk\,\,k^{2}\,\frac{1}{k}\frac{2}{e^{\beta k}-1}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\,\lambda\,\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,dk\,k\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta k}-1}

(171)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda T^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\,\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,\frac{dx\,\,x}{e^{x}-1}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,[{\rm{Assumed}}\,\,\,x=\beta k]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda T^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\,\zeta(2)=\frac{\lambda T^{2}}{24}.

where we have used

\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,\frac{dx\,\,x^{n-1}}{e^{x}-1}=(n-1)!\zeta(n).

(172)

We note that
\Pi=\lambda T^{2}/24
will act as a thermal mass of the scalar field at finite temperature. We will discuss this in details
when the dispersion property of a particle at finite
T
will be discussed later.

5.2
One-Loop Self-Energy in
\phi^{3}
-Theory

.

We consider three scalar fields
\phi
,
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
which differ by masses. The interaction Lagrangian density is given by

{\cal L}_{I}=-\lambda\phi(x)\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x)\,,

(173)

where
\lambda
is the interaction strength.

Figure 13:
Scalar self-energy diagram in
\phi^{3}
-theory at
T\neq 0
.

Our aim is to compute the diagram in Fig.
13
, which occurs typically in one-loop approximation
of the self-energy of the field
\phi
. The self-energy can be written from Fig.
13
as

\Pi_{2}(K)=\int\frac{d^{4}P}{(2\pi)^{4}}(-i\lambda)\frac{i}{P^{2}-m_{1}^{2}}(-i\lambda)\frac{i}{(P-K)^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}\,,

(174)

where
P
is the momentum of the
\phi_{1}
field with mass
m_{1}
,
P-K
is the momentum of the
\phi_{2}
field with mass
m_{2}
. The self-energy can be
written as

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}(k_{0},k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}T\sum_{p_{0}}\frac{1}{(p_{0}^{2}-E_{p}^{2})[(p_{0}-k_{0})^{2}-E_{p-k}^{2}]}

(175)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}T\sum_{p_{0}}f(p_{0}=i\omega_{n},k_{0}=i\omega_{m})\,,

where
E_{p}^{2}=p^{2}+m_{1}^{2}
and
E^{2}_{p-k}=(p-k)^{2}+m_{2}^{2}
.
Now the frequency sum over
p_{0}
should be replaced by the contour integral given in (
139
) as

\displaystyle T\sum_{p_{0}}f(p_{0}=i\omega_{n},k_{0}=i\omega_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{1}{2}\left[f(p_{0})+f(-p_{0})\right]

(176)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{f(p_{0})+f(-p_{0})}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I^{0}_{1}+I^{0}_{2}+I^{\beta}_{3}+I^{\beta}_{4}\,.

Calculation of
I^{0}_{1}
:

Figure 14:
Contour corresponding to the integral
I_{1}^{0}
in complex
p_{0}
plane.

\displaystyle I^{0}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}f(p_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{1}{(p_{0}^{2}-E_{p}^{2})[(p_{0}-k_{0})^{2}-E_{p-k}^{2}]}\,,

(177)

which has four poles at
p_{0}=\pm E_{p}
and
k_{0}\pm E_{p-k}
with
k_{0}=ik_{4}=i\omega_{m}
. The contour is in right half plane as shown in Fig.
14

from the definition of the conversion of frequency sum to contour integral. Therefore,

\displaystyle I^{0}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}f(p_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times\frac{1}{2}\times 2\pi i\times{\textrm{sum of residues}}

(178)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\left.R_{1}^{0}\right|_{p_{0}=E_{p}}+\left.R^{0}_{2}\right|_{p_{0}=k_{0}+E_{p-k}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p}-k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p-k}}+\frac{1}{2E_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p-k}+k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p}}\right]\,.

Calculation of
I^{0}_{2}
:

Figure 15:
Contour corresponding to the integral
I_{2}^{0}
in complex
p_{0}
plane.

\displaystyle I^{0}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}f(-p_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{1}{(p_{0}^{2}-E_{p}^{2})[(p_{0}+k_{0})^{2}-E_{p-k}^{2}]}\,,

(179)

which has four poles at
p_{0}=\pm E_{p}
and
-k_{0}\pm E_{p-k}
. The contour is in left half plane as shown in Fig.
15
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle I^{0}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{1}{2}f(-p_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times\frac{1}{2}\times(-2\pi i)\times{\textrm{sum of residues}}\,,

(180)

where negative sign in the right hand side is due to the contour in clockwise direction. We can now write

\displaystyle I^{0}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\left.R_{1}^{0^{\prime}}\right|_{p_{0}=-E_{p}}+\left.R^{0^{\prime}}_{2}\right|_{p_{0}=-k_{0}-E_{p-k}}\right]

(181)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p}-k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p-k}}+\frac{1}{2E_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p-k}+k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p}}\right]\,.

Calculation of
I^{\beta}_{3}
:

Figure 16:
Contour corresponding to the integral
I_{3}^{\beta}
in complex
p_{0}
plane.

\displaystyle I^{\beta}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{f(p_{0})}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{1}{(p_{0}^{2}-E_{p}^{2})[(p_{0}-k_{0})^{2}-E_{p-k}^{2}]}\frac{1}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}\,,

(182)

which has four poles at
p_{0}=\pm E_{p}
and
k_{0}\pm E_{p-k}
. The contour is in right half plane as shown Fig.
16
. Therefore,

\displaystyle I^{\beta}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{f(p_{0})}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times 2\pi i\times{\textrm{sum of residues}}

(183)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\left.R^{\beta}_{3}\right|_{p_{0}=E_{p}}+\left.R^{\beta}_{4}\right|_{p_{0}=k_{0}+E_{p-k}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p}-k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta E_{p}}-1}+\frac{1}{2E_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p-k}+k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta(k_{0}+E_{p-k})}-1}\right]\,.

Using
k_{0}=ik_{4}=i\omega_{m}=2\pi imT
we get

e^{\beta(k_{0}+E_{p-k})}-1=e^{\beta E_{p-k}}-1,

(184)

as
e^{k_{0}\beta}=e^{2\pi im}=1
. Now we can write

\displaystyle I^{\beta}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p}-k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta E_{p}}-1}+\frac{1}{2E_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p-k}+k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta E_{p-k}}-1}\right]\,.

(185)

Calculation of
I^{\beta}_{4}
:

Figure 17:
Contour corresponding to the integral
I_{4}^{\beta}
in complex
p_{0}
plane.

\displaystyle I^{\beta}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{f(-p_{0})}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{1}{(p_{0}^{2}-E_{p}^{2})[(p_{0}+k_{0})^{2}-E_{p-k}^{2}]}\frac{1}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}\,,

(186)

which has four poles at
p_{0}=\pm E_{p}
and
-k_{0}\pm E_{p-k}
. The contour is in right half plane as shown in Fig.
17
. Therefore,

\displaystyle I^{\beta}_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon+i\infty}dp_{0}\frac{f(-p_{0})}{e^{\beta p_{0}}-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\times 2\pi i\times{\textrm{sum of residues}}

(187)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\left.R_{3}^{\beta^{\prime}}\right|_{p_{0}=E_{p}}+\left.R^{\beta^{\prime}}_{4}\right|_{p_{0}=-k_{0}+E_{p-k}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p}+k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta E_{p}}-1}+\frac{1}{2E_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p-k}-k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta(E_{p-k}-k_{0})}-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{1}{2E_{p}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p}+k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta E_{p}}-1}+\frac{1}{2E_{p-k}}\,\frac{1}{(E_{p-k}-k_{0})^{2}-E^{2}_{p}}\,\frac{1}{e^{\beta E_{p-k}}-1}\right]\,.

Using (
176
), (
178
), (
181
), (
185
) and (
187
) in (
175
), one can have the self-energy expression as

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=i\omega_{m},k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda^{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{4E_{p}E_{p-k}}

(188)

\displaystyle\left[\left(1+n_{B}(E_{p})+n_{B}(E_{p-k})\right)\left(\frac{1}{k_{0}-E_{p}-E_{p-k}}-\frac{1}{k_{0}+E_{p}+E_{p-k}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-(n_{B}(E_{p})-n_{B}(E_{p-k}))\left(\frac{1}{k_{0}-E_{p}+E_{p-k}}-\frac{1}{k_{0}+E_{p}-E_{p-k}}\right)\right]\,,

where
n_{B}(E_{i})=1/(e^{\beta E_{i}}-1)
. The terms
1/(k_{0}\pm E_{p}\pm E_{p-k})
and
1/(k_{0}\mp E_{p}\pm E_{p-k})
are the Landau damping factors.
We note the following points on (
188
):

\bullet

It is to be noted that the Bose-Einstein distribution function
n_{B}(E_{i})
appearing in
\Pi_{2}(k_{0},k)
involves on-shell energies
E_{P}
and
E_{p-k}

of the internal lines of the self-energy but the energies of the internal lines should be off-shell. This implies that there should be cut or discontinuity in
\Pi_{2}(k_{0},k)
.

\bullet

\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=i\omega_{m},k)
is defined for discrete imaginary values of
k_{0}=i\omega_{m}=2\pi imT
.
One could make analytic continuation in whole complex plane by putting
k_{0}=i\omega_{m}\rightarrow\omega+i\eta
if
\Pi^{*}_{2}(k_{0}=i\omega_{m},k)=\Pi_{2}(k^{*}_{0},k)
.

\bullet

It is easy to see that the analytic extension has cuts along the real axis and the discontinuity along the cuts is pure imaginary:

\textrm{Disc}\,\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=i\omega_{m},k)=\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=\omega+i\eta,k)-\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=\omega-i\eta,k)=2i\,\textrm{Im}\,\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=\omega+i\eta,k)\,.

(189)

\bullet

The discontinuity can easily be obtained by finding out
\textrm{Im}\,\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=\omega+i\eta,k)
. Using the relation

\textrm{Im}\,f(q_{0}=\omega+i\eta,q)=\textrm{Im}\,\left(\frac{1}{q_{0}+q\mp\eta}\right)=\pm\pi\delta(q_{0}+q)=-\textrm{Sgn}(\eta)\pi\delta(q_{0}+q)\,,

(190)

one can find the
\textrm{Im}\,\Pi_{2}(k_{0}=\omega+i\eta,k)
as

\displaystyle\textrm{Im}\,\Pi_{2}(\omega,k)=\pi\lambda^{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{4E_{p}E_{p-k}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Big[\Big\{(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))-n_{B}(E_{p})n_{B}(E_{p-k})\Big\}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Big\{\delta(\omega-E_{p}-E_{p-k})-\delta(\omega+E_{p}+E_{p-k})\Big\}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Big\{n_{B}(E_{p})(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))-n_{B}(E_{p-k})(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))\Big\}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Big\{\delta(\omega-E_{p}+E_{p-k})-\delta(\omega+E_{p}-E_{p-k})\Big\}\Big].

Figure 18:
Feynman diagram for decay process in
\phi^{3}
-theory at
T=0
.

\bullet

At
T=0

\textrm{Im}\,\Pi_{2}(\omega,k)=\pi\lambda^{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{4E_{p}E_{p-k}}\Big\{\delta(\omega-E_{p}-E_{p-k})-\delta(\omega+E_{p}+E_{p-k})\Big\}.

The first term with energy conserving
\delta(\omega-E_{p}-E_{p-k})
indicates a decay process
\phi\rightarrow\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}
as shown in Fig.
18
.
The energy conserving
\delta(\omega+E_{p}+E_{p-k})
in the second term will never be satisfied and hence does not correspond to any physical process.

\bullet

At
T\neq 0
the available phase space is weighted by the distribution function.
There will also be additional processes compared to
T=0
case above. The processes are related by principle of detailed balance.

Figure 19:
Feynman diagram for various processes in
\phi^{3}
-theory at
T\neq 0
.

(a) Consider the first term in (
):

\Big[(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))-n_{B}(E_{p})n_{B}(E_{p-k})\Big]\delta(\omega-E_{p}-E_{p-k})\,.

The term
(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))\delta(\omega-E_{p}-E_{p-k})
indicates a decay process
\phi\rightarrow\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}
in Fig.
19
(a) similar to
T=0
case but modified by thermal weight factor. The term
n_{B}(E_{p})n_{B}(E_{p-k})\delta(\omega-E_{p}-E_{p-k})
indicates a reverse process
\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}\rightarrow\phi

in Fig.
19
(b) which was not there in
T=0
case.

Figure 20:
Feynman diagram for various processes in
\phi^{3}
-theory at
T\neq 0
.

(b) Consider the third term in (
):

\Big[n_{B}(E_{p})(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))-n_{B}(E_{p-k})(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))\Big]\delta(\omega-E_{p}+E_{p-k})\,.

The term
n_{B}(E_{p})(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))\delta(\omega-E_{p}+E_{p-k})
indicates a absorption process
\phi+\phi_{2}\rightarrow\phi_{1}
in Fig.
20
(a) . The term

n_{B}(E_{p-k})(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))\delta(\omega-E_{p}+E_{p-k})
indicates an emission process
\phi_{1}\rightarrow\phi+\phi_{2}
in Fig.
20
(b) . These process were not there in
T=0
case.

(c) Consider the fourth term in (
):

\Big[n_{B}(E_{p})(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))-n_{B}(E_{p-k})(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))\Big]\delta(\omega+E_{p}-E_{p-k})\,.

The term
n_{B}(E_{p})(1+n_{B}(E_{p-k}))\delta(\omega+E_{p}-E_{p-k})
indicates a absorption process
\phi+\phi_{1}\rightarrow\phi_{2}
as shown in Fig.
21
(a) whereas
the term
n_{B}(E_{p-k})(1+n_{B}(E_{p}))\delta(\omega+E_{p}-E_{p-k})
implies an emission process
\phi_{2}\rightarrow\phi+\phi_{1}
in Fig.
21
(b) . These process were not there in
T=0
case.

Figure 21:
Feynman diagram for various processes in
\phi^{3}
-theory at
T\neq 0
.

6
Partition Function

Using (
90
) the density matrix of the system in (
85
) becomes

\displaystyle\rho(\beta)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle e^{-\beta{\cal H}}=\rho_{0}(\beta)S(\beta)=e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\,{\cal T}\left(e^{{-\int_{0}^{\beta}}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau}\right)

(192)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\,\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\,{\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)^{l}.

Using this the partition function in (
32
) becomes

\displaystyle{\cal Z}(\beta)={\rm{Tr}}\rho(\beta)\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left[e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\,\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\,{\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)^{l}\,\,\right]

(193)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left[e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\,+\,e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\ \sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\,{\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)^{l}\,\,\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left[e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\right]\,+{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\ \sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\,{\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)^{l}\,\,\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}+{\cal Z}_{I}^{l},

where the free (
{\cal Z}_{0}
) and the interaction (
{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}
) pieces are separated out by expanding around the free piece.

The logarithm of the partition function is of interest as far as thermodynamic quantities are concerned. We can write as

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[{\cal Z}_{0}+{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}\right]=\ln\left\{{\cal Z}_{0}\left[1+\frac{{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\right]\right\}=\ln{\cal Z}_{0}+\ln\left[1+\frac{{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\right],

(194)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}+\left[\frac{{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\,\,-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\right)^{2}\,+\cdots\right].

where

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[1+\frac{{\cal Z}_{I}^{l}}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\right]=\ln\left(1+\frac{{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\ \sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\,{\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)^{l}\,\,\right]}{{\rm{Tr}}\left[e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\right]}\right)

(195)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left(1+\sum_{l}\frac{1}{l!}\,\left\langle{\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)^{l}\right\rangle_{0}\right)=\ln\left[1+\sum_{l}\frac{\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{l}\right\rangle_{0}}{l!}\right],

where
\langle\cdots\rangle_{0}
is the expectation value with respect to non-interacting ensemble. We know that
{\cal H}^{\prime}
contains one power of coupling in the theory.
Now an expansion of
\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{l}\right\rangle_{0}
up to 3rd order in
l
(
=1,2,3
) means an expansion of
\ln{\cal Z}_{I}
up to third order in coupling,
which can then be written as

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{I}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\ln\left[1+\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{2}\right\rangle_{0}+\frac{1}{6}\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{3}\right\rangle_{0}\right]=\ln(1+x).

(196)

Again expanding
\ln(1+x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\ (-1)^{j+1}x^{j}/j
, one can write

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{I}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle x-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{3}x^{3}+\cdots

(197)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{2}\right\rangle_{0}-\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{6}\left(\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{3}\right\rangle_{0}-3\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}\,\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{2}\right\rangle_{0}+2\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}+\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{2}+\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{3},

where we have assembled the terms according to the power of coupling. Using (
197
) in (
194
), the perturbative expansion
of the partition function becomes

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}+\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}+\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{2}+\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{3},

(198)

where

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}\,,

(199)

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{2}\right\rangle_{0}-\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}\right)\,,

(200)

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\left(\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{3}\right\rangle_{0}-3\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}\,\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}^{2}\right\rangle_{0}+2\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}^{3}\right).

(201)

This is a perturbative expansion of the partition function around the free theory and one needs to compute it in order by order
of the coupling strength of a given theory. As discussed earlier that the Trace in (
193
) stands for sum over expectation values of all
possible states in Hilbert space and there are infinite number of such states in quantum field theory. So, for an interacting system the partition
function will extremely be tedious to compute even if one expands in perturbation series in interaction strength in a given theory.
It would be convenient to compute
the partition function in functional or path integral approach.

6.1
Relation of Functional Integration and the Partition Function

Since we will be dealing with statistical thermodynamics problem when the system returns to its initial state after a time evolution from
t=0
to
t
, the corresponding
transition can be written in a functional form as
\langle\phi_{a}|e^{-i{\cal H}t}|\phi_{a}\rangle
, assuming the Hamiltonian is time independent that simplifies
the transition amplitude from one state to other.

The transition amplitude in Minkowski space-time is obtained in functional integration approach in (
68
) as

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{a}|e^{-i{\cal H}t}|\phi_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\phi_{a}(\bm{\vec{x}})}^{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},t)=\phi_{a}(\bm{\vec{x}})}{\cal D}\phi\ e^{i\int\limits_{0}^{t_{f}}dt\int d^{3}x\ {\cal L}(\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},t),{\dot{\phi}(\bm{\vec{x}},t)})}=\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\phi_{a}(\bm{\vec{x}})}^{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},t)=\phi_{a}(\bm{\vec{x}})}{\cal D}\phi\ e^{iS[\phi]}\,,

(202)

where
S[\phi]
is the action of a system. This is the so-called path integral, and
where
\cal D
is the functional or path integral runs over all possible paths of the field
\phi(x)
. These fields are restricted by
boundary conditions while going from initial time
t=0
to final time
t_{f}=t
as discussed in subsec
2.3
.
The action in Minkowski space-time is written as

{\cal S}[\phi]=\int d^{4}{X}\,{\cal L}=\int\limits_{0}^{t}\,dt\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}.

(203)

Now, the partition function in (
5
) reads as

{\cal Z}={\rm{Tr}}\rho\ ={\rm{Tr}}\left(e^{-\beta{\cal H}}\right)=\sum_{n}\ \ \left\langle n\left|e^{-\beta{\cal H}}\right|n\right\rangle\ \ ,

(204)

where the summation over
n
includes all the possible energy eigenstates of the system in Hilbert space.
In the continuum case the summation becomes an integral, and the eigenstates
|\phi\rangle

form a complete set, each with energy
E_{\phi}
. Thus, the partition function becomes

{\cal Z}=\int\ d\phi\ \left\langle\phi\left|e^{-\beta{\cal H}}\right|\phi\right\rangle\left(=\int d\phi e^{-\beta E_{\phi}}\right)\ .

(205)

If one compares (
202
) and (
205
), there is a striking similarity between the path integral formulation of the transition amplitude
in quantum field theory and the partition function in statistical mechanics provided that

1.

the Boltzmann factor
e^{-\beta{\cal H}}
acquires the form of a time
evaluation operator (
e^{-i{\cal H}t}
) for imaginary time (
\beta=it
) through analytic continuation.

2.

the time interval
[0,t]
in the transition amplitude in (
202
) takes the role of
\beta
in the
partition function with interval
[0,\beta
] along with
\tau=it
. This is known as
Wick rotation

that rotates the integration by
90^{\circ}
in complex plane as displayed in Fig.
4
.

3.

the field
\phi
obey periodic or anti-periodic boundary condition,
\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\pm\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)
,
as discussed earlier in subsec.
3.5
.

With this the transition amplitude can be regarded as the partition function in path integral approach as

\displaystyle{\cal Z}={\rm{Tr}}\rho\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left(e^{-\beta{\cal H}}\right)=\int\ d\phi\ \left\langle\phi\left|e^{-\beta{\cal H}}\right|\phi\right\rangle=\int{\cal D}\phi\ e^{i\int\limits_{0}^{t}\,dt\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}}

(206)

\displaystyle{=\atop t\rightarrow-i\tau}

\displaystyle\,\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\pm\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)}{\cal D}\phi\ e^{\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d(it)\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}(t\rightarrow-i\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\pm\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)}{\cal D}\phi\ e^{\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}(t\rightarrow-i\tau)}.

One can compute the partition function in Euclidean time
\tau
and discrete frequency
i\omega_{n}
directly using (
206
).

6.2
Scalar Field Partition Function

6.2.1
Partition function for free real scalar field

A real non-interacting scalar field Lagrangian is given in (
166
) by

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\right)^{2}-({\mathbf{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}-m^{2}\phi^{2}\right],

(207)

in Minkowski space-time.
This can be written only in Euclidean time
Kapusta
;
Schmitt
;
Yang
;
Inga
as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{0}(t\rightarrow-i\tau)

\displaystyle{=\atop{t\rightarrow-i\tau}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\left({\partial_{t}\phi}\right)^{2}-({\mathbf{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}-m^{2}\phi^{2}\right]=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\left({\partial_{\tau}\phi}\right)^{2}+({\mathbf{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2}\right]\,.

(208)

The Fourier transform of the field
\phi(X)
can be written as

\displaystyle\phi(X)=\phi(x,\tau)

\displaystyle{=\atop{t\rightarrow-i\tau}}\atop{k_{0}\rightarrow i\omega_{n}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{K}\,e^{-iK\cdot X}\,\phi(K)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{K}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{-i(i\omega_{n})(-i\tau)}\,\phi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})

(209)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{K}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,e^{-i\omega_{n}\tau}\,\phi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}}),

where
\sum_{K}=\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}
and
V
is the three volume.

Using (
208
) in (
206
), one can write the partition function for free scalar field as

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)}{\cal D}\phi\ e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\left[\left({\partial_{\tau}\phi}\right)^{2}+({\mathbf{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2}\right]},

(210)

where scalar field obeys periodicity condition.

Now we calculate explicitly the terms in the exponential of (
210
):

First term:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\int d^{3}{x}\,\left(\partial_{\tau}\phi\right)^{2}=\int d^{4}X\,\frac{1}{V\beta}\sum_{K}\,\sum_{K^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\tau}\left[e^{-i\omega_{n}\tau}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\phi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\right]

(211)

\displaystyle\times\partial_{\tau}\left[e^{-i\omega_{m}\tau}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{x}}}\phi(\omega_{m},\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}X\,\frac{1}{V\beta}\sum_{K}\,\sum_{K^{\prime}}\,e^{i(\bm{\vec{k}}+\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})\cdot\bm{\bm{x}}}e^{-i(\omega_{n}+\omega_{m})\tau}\phi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})(-i\omega_{n})(-i\omega_{m})\phi(\omega_{m},\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{V\beta}\sum_{K}\sum_{K^{\prime}}\left[V\delta^{3}(\bm{\vec{k}}+\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})\right]\left[\beta\delta(-\omega_{n}-\omega_{m})\right]\phi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})(-i\omega_{n})(-i\omega_{m})\phi(\omega_{m},\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{K}\ \,\phi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\,\omega_{n}^{2}\phi(-\omega_{n},-\bm{\vec{k}})=\sum_{K}\,\omega_{n}^{2}\,\phi(K)\,\phi(-K)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{K}\,\omega_{n}^{2}\,\phi^{*}(K)\,\phi(K),

where we have used
\phi(K)=\phi^{*}(-K)
since
\phi(X)
is real in (
209
). We have also used of the following relations:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\ e^{i(-\omega_{n}-\omega_{m})\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\delta(-\omega_{n}-\omega_{m})\,,

(212)

\displaystyle\int d^{3}x\ e^{i(\bm{\vec{k}}+\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})\cdot x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\delta^{3}(\bm{\vec{k}}+\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})\,,

(213)

\displaystyle\omega_{-n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi n}{\beta}=-\omega_{n}\,.

(214)

Second term:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\int d^{3}{x}\,\left(\mathbf{\nabla}\phi\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{K}{k}^{2}\phi^{*}(K)\,\phi(K).

(215)

Third term:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}d\tau\int d^{3}{x}\,\,m^{2}\phi^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{K}\,m^{2}\,\phi^{*}(K)\,\phi(K)\,.

(216)

Using (
211
), (
215
) and (
216
) one can write

\displaystyle{-\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\left[\left({\partial_{\tau}\phi}\right)^{2}+({\mathbf{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{K}\,\,\phi^{*}(K)\Big[k_{0}^{2}+{k}^{2}+m^{2}\Big]\phi(K)

(217)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{K}\,\,\phi^{*}(K)\Big[k_{0}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}\Big]\phi(K)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{K}\,\phi^{*}(K)\,{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)\phi(K)\,,

where
{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}=(\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2})
is the free inverse propagator in Euclidean time with energy
\omega_{k}=\sqrt{k^{2}+m^{2}}
.

Using (
217
) in (
210
) one can write the free scalar field partition function as

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}\,\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)}{\cal D}\phi\ \exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{K}\,\phi^{*}(K)\,[{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)]\phi(K)\right]

(218)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{K}\int\limits_{\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=\phi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)}{\cal D}\phi\ \exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,\phi^{*}(K)\,\,[{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)]\,\,\phi(K)\right].

We also note that inverse propagator is in general a diagonal matrix.

The integral can be performed using the standard
identity
5
5

5

This identity is a generalisation of the one dimensional Gaussian integral

\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\ \ \exp(-\frac{1}{2}ay^{2})=\sqrt{2\pi/a}
and can be shown by expressing
the bilinear
{\mathbf{y}}\cdot{\widehat{A}}{\mathbf{y}}
in terms of eigenvalues of
\widehat{A}
.

\displaystyle\int d^{D}y\,\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\,{\mathbf{y}}\cdot{\widehat{A}}{\mathbf{y}}}=(2\pi)^{D/2}({\mbox{det}}{\widehat{A}})^{-1/2},

(219)

for hermitian positive definite matrix
\widehat{A}
and
D
is the dimension of the system. The partition function can now be written as

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N\left[{\mbox{det}}{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(220)

where the determinant is taken over momentum space as
{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)
is diagonal.
We also note that the constant factor is absorbed in
N
, which is irrelevant as it is temperature independent. Now the
logarithm of a partition function up to a constant, becomes

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[{\mbox{det}}[{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)]\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}

(221)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\ln\prod_{K}[{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)]=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{K}\ln[{\cal G}^{-1}_{0}(K)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n,{k}}\ln(\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\,V\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\sum_{n}\ln(\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}).

Below we perform the sum integral as

\displaystyle T\sum_{n}\frac{1}{T}\ln\left(\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}\right)=T\sum_{k-0}\frac{1}{T}\ln\left(-k_{0}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}\right)=T\sum_{k_{0}}\frac{1}{T}\left[\ln\left(\omega_{k}-k_{0}\right)+\ln\left(\omega_{k}+k_{0}\right)\right],

(222)

where
\omega=i\omega_{n}=k_{0}
.

Now concentrating on the first term: using (
134
) we can write

\displaystyle T\sum_{k_{0}}\frac{1}{T}\ln\left(\omega_{k}-k_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\ln\left(\omega_{k}-k_{0}\right)\frac{1}{2}\ \coth\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

(223)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{-1}{\omega_{k}-k_{0}}\right)\int dk_{0}\coth\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{k}-k_{0}}\right)\int dk_{0}\coth\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{k}-k_{0}}\right)\frac{2}{\beta}\ln\left[\sinh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\omega_{k}-k_{0}}\right)\ln\left[\sinh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[\sinh\left(\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}\right)\right].

Similarly for the second term: choosing the opposite contour as the first one we get,

\displaystyle T\sum_{k_{0}}\ln\left(\omega_{k}+k_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[\sinh\left(\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}\right)\right],

(224)

we note here that there will be a term of
i\pi
in (
224
), which we neglect as it would make partition function imaginary.
So, the results of frequency sum in (
222
) can be written as,

\displaystyle T\sum_{n}\frac{1}{T}\ln\left(\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\ln\left[\sinh\left(\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}\right)\right]

(225)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\omega_{k}-2\ln 2+2\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right).

We used

\displaystyle\sinh\left(\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}}-e^{\frac{-\beta\omega_{k}}{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}e^{\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}}\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\therefore\ln\left[\sinh\left(\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}-\ln 2+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right).

(226)

Now using (
225
) in (
221
) one gets logarithm of the partition function

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-V\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\left[\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}-\ln 2+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)\right],

(227)

where again
\ln 2
can be neglected as it is independent of temperature. This agrees with the result obtained in (
16
).

The free energy density for free scalar field can be obtained from (
6a
) as

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T\ln{\cal Z}_{0}\,=VT\,\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\left[\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)\right],

(228)

In the infinite volume limit the pressure for free scalar field can be obtained from (
6b
) as

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\Omega}_{0}}{V}=T\,\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\left[-\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}-\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)\right],

(229)

where the first term is the zero temperature part.

6.2.2
Partition function for interacting scalar field

The interaction Lagrangian density as given in (
166
) as

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4},

(230)

The partition function in first order
\lambda
as given in (
199
)

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle{\cal F}_{I}\right\rangle_{0}=\frac{1}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\,{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,e^{-\beta{\cal H}_{0}}\ {\cal T}\left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}{\cal H}^{\prime}\ d\tau\right)\right].

(231)

Using path integral it becomes
Kapusta

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int{\cal D}\phi\ {\cal S}_{I}\,e^{i{\cal S}_{0}[\phi]}}{\int{\cal D}\phi\ e^{i{\cal S}_{0}[\phi]}}

(232)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi\ \left(-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}_{I}({t\rightarrow-i\tau})\right)\,e^{-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}_{0}({t\rightarrow-i\tau})}}{\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi\ e^{-\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}_{0}({t\rightarrow-i\tau})}},

where free partition function is already calculated in (
218
) as

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\ \exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{n,k}\,[\omega^{2}_{n}+\omega_{k}^{2}]\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\right].

(233)

Now we need to compute the numerator of (
232
). Using the same method as the free case one can proceed
as
Kapusta
;
Yang

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal Z}_{0}}\,\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\ \left(-\lambda\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\phi^{4}\right)\,\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{n,k}\,[\omega^{2}_{n}+\omega_{k}^{2}]\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\right].

(234)

Using the Fourier decomposition of fields in (
209
), one can get

\displaystyle\lambda\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\phi^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{(V\beta)^{2}}\,\sum_{m_{1},\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}}\sum_{m_{2},\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}}\sum_{m_{3},\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}}\sum_{m_{4},\bm{\vec{q}_{4}}}\,\phi_{m_{1},\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}}\,\phi_{m_{2},\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}}\,\phi_{m_{3},\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}}\,\phi_{m_{4},\bm{\vec{q}_{4}}}

(235)

\displaystyle\times\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\,e^{i(\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}+\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}+\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}+\bm{\vec{q}_{4}})\cdot{x}}\,\,e^{-i(\omega_{m_{1}}+\omega_{m_{2}}+\omega_{m_{3}}+\omega_{m_{4}})\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{(V\beta)^{2}}\,\sum_{m_{1},\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}}\sum_{m_{2},\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}}\sum_{m_{3},\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}}\sum_{m_{4},\bm{\vec{q}_{4}}}\,\phi_{m_{1},\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}}\,\phi_{m_{2},\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}}\,\phi_{m_{3},\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}}\,\phi_{m_{4},\bm{\vec{q}_{4}}}

\displaystyle\times\beta\delta(-\omega_{m_{1}}-\omega_{m_{2}}-\omega_{m_{3}}-\omega_{m_{4}})\ V\ \delta^{3}(\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}+\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}+\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}+\bm{\vec{q}_{4}}).

We have used two delta functions following (
212
) and (
213
) from
\tau
and
x
integrations, respectively and
they guarantee the energy momentum conservation in the interaction vertex. Now, the non-zero contribution comes
when
\omega_{m_{1}}=-\omega_{m_{2}}
,
\omega_{m_{3}}=-\omega_{m_{4}}
and
\bm{\vec{q}_{1}}=-\bm{\vec{q}_{2}}
,
\bm{\vec{q}_{3}}=-\bm{\vec{q}_{4}}
.
Obviously, there are another two combination that would give non-zero contributions.
This allows 3 non-zero permutations among
\omega_{m_{i}}
and
{q}_{i}
. Then one can write

\displaystyle\lambda\,\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\phi^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\lambda\,(\beta V)\,\left(\frac{1}{V\beta}\right)^{2}\,\sum_{m,\bm{\vec{q}}}\sum_{l,\bm{\vec{p}}}\,\left|\phi_{m,\bm{\vec{q}}}\right|^{2}\,\left|\phi_{l,\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|^{2}.

(236)

Using (
236
) in (
234
), one gets

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\lambda\beta V\left(\frac{1}{V\beta}\right)^{2}\sum_{m,\bm{\vec{q}}}\sum_{l,\bm{\vec{p}}}\frac{{\prod\atop{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}}\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\ \left|\phi_{m,\bm{\vec{q}}}\right|^{2}\,\left|\phi_{l,\bm{\vec{p}}}\right|^{2}\,\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,(\omega^{2}_{n}+\omega_{k}^{2})\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\right]}{{\prod\atop{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}}\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\ \exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,(\omega^{2}_{n}+\omega_{k}^{2})\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\right]}

(237)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\lambda\beta V\left(\frac{1}{V\beta}\right)^{2}\left[\sum_{n\bm{\vec{,}k}}\frac{\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\,\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}(\omega^{2}_{n}+\omega_{k}^{2})\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\right]}{\int_{\tiny{\mbox{\sf periodic}}}{\cal D}\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,(\omega^{2}_{n}+\omega_{k}^{2})\left|\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\right|^{2}\right]}\right]^{2},

if
m=l=n
and
\bm{\vec{q}}=\bm{\vec{p}}=\bm{\vec{k}}
, the integral over
\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}
gets factorised as above.
When
m\neq l\neq n
and
\bm{\vec{q}}\neq\bm{\vec{p}}\neq\bm{\vec{k}}
, the
\phi_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}

integral in the numerator and denominator are identical and they cancel out, thus the integral disappears.

Using Gaussian integral
\int dy\ y^{2n}\,e^{-\alpha y^{2}}=\Gamma(n+1/2)/\alpha^{n+1/2}
, one gets

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\,\lambda\,\beta V\ \left(\frac{1}{V\beta}\right)^{2}\left[\frac{\Gamma(1+\frac{1}{2})}{\alpha^{3/2}}\times\frac{\alpha^{1/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}\right]^{2}=-3\,\lambda\,\beta V\ \left(\frac{1}{V\beta}\right)^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2\alpha}\right]^{2}

(238)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\,\lambda\,\beta V\ \left(\frac{1}{V\beta}\right)^{2}\left[\sum_{n,k}\frac{1}{(\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2})}\right]^{2}=-3\,\lambda\,\beta V\left[\frac{1}{V\beta}\sum_{n,k}\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}}\right]^{2}.

Taking continuum limit
\sum_{k}\rightarrow V\,\int d^{3}{k}/(2\pi)^{3}
, one gets

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\,\lambda\,\beta V\left[\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{2}+\omega_{k}^{2}}\right]^{2}.

(239)

where the
\sum_{n}
is Euclidean. Casting this into Minkowski
\omega_{n}=k_{4}=-ik_{0}
, one can write

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\ 3\,\lambda\,\beta V\left[\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{k_{0}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\right]^{2},

(240)

where one can use contour integration in (
131
) vis-a-vis (
134
) and (
136
).
We also note the following points:

\bullet

The term inside the square braces are the sum-integral that comes from the loop integral
\int d^{4}K/(2\pi)^{4}
and
the scalar propagator (
1/(k_{0}^{2}-{k}^{2}-m^{2})=1/(k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2})
with energy
\omega_{k}=\sqrt{{k}^{2}+m^{2}}
.

\bullet

\lambda
is the interaction vertex.

\bullet

\beta V
is the left out factor that comes from the energy-momentum conservation (e.g., (
235
)) in the vertex.

\bullet

The square,
[\cdots]^{2}
, indicates that two loops connected to one interaction point
\lambda
.

\Rightarrow

All these together correspond to a topologically distinct diagram
[
]
.

\bullet

3
is the symmetry factor that comes from 3 different permutation of contraction allowed by the energy-momentum conservation.

Now the first order correction to the scalar partition function in (
240
) can be represented in Feynman diagram as

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\ 3\,\beta V\times{\includegraphics[height,width]{vaccum_bubble.pdf}}.

(241)

The frequency sum in (
240
) is exactly similar to that of tadpole diagram as done in Subsec.
5.1
but with different symmetry
factor (
1/2
). Excluding this
1/2
factor and
\lambda
and the zero temperature part, the result of the sum integral can be obtained
from (
171
) as
T^{2}/12
. Thus, the first
order correction to the scalar partition function in (
240
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\ 3\,\lambda\,\beta V\left[\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2}-\omega_{k}^{2}}\right]^{2}=-\ 3\,\lambda\,\beta V\ \frac{T^{4}}{144}=-\ \lambda\,\frac{VT^{3}}{48}.

(242)

6.2.3
Pressure

The logarithm of the scalar partition function up to first order in coupling can now be written
using (
194
), (
227
) and (
242
) as

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}+\left(\ln{\cal Z}_{I}\right)_{1}

(243)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-V\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)-\ \lambda\,\frac{VT^{3}}{48}+{\cal O}(\lambda^{2}),

where we have also dropped the
T=0
contribution in
\ln{\cal Z}_{0}
. In the infinite volume limit the pressure up to
first order can be obtained as

\displaystyle{\cal P}=-\frac{\Omega}{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T\,\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)-\ \lambda\,\frac{T^{4}}{48}+{\cal O}(\lambda^{2})

(244)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}T^{4}}{90}-\ \lambda\,\frac{T^{4}}{48}+{\cal O}(\lambda^{2}),

where
\Omega
is the thermodynamic potential. The other thermodynamic quantities can be obtained from pressure.
One can also compute the higher order corrections to the partition function following (
200
) and (
201
),
and thus higher order thermodynamic quantities.

6.3
Fermion Field

Until now we have discussed partition function for real scalar field and it’s pressure up to first order
in coupling. In this section we will compute the partition function for free fermionic fields. The computation of
interacting fermionic partition function is postponed until we introduce gauge theory, quantum electrodynamics (QED).
Since fermions are anticommuting fields, they are Grassmann variables. We start this section by reviewing some of
the properties of Fermionic fields.

6.3.1
Fermionic Lagrangian and conserved charge

The Lagrangian density that describes the non-interacting fermion is given in Minkowski space-time
pbpal
;
Peskin
as

\displaystyle{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bar{\psi}}\left(i\partial\!\!\!/\penalty-m\right)\psi,

(245)

where
m
is the mass of the fermion and the fields
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
are to be treated independently.
The
\gamma
-matrices in Dirac-Pauli representation are given as

\displaystyle\gamma^{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}I&0\\
0&-I\end{array}\right)\hskip 21.68121pt{\mbox{and}}\hskip 21.68121pt\gamma^{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\sigma^{i}\\
-\sigma^{i}&0\end{array}\right)\,,

where
I
is a
2\times 2
unit matrix and the Pauli matrices
\sigma^{i}
’s are

\displaystyle\sigma^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 14.45377pt\sigma^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&-i\\
i&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 14.45377pt{\mbox{and}}\hskip 14.45377pt\sigma^{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)\,.

Using the Euler-Lagrange equation for
\bar{\psi}
field

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial\bar{\psi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\partial_{\mu}}\left(\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial(\partial^{\mu}\bar{\psi})}\right)\Rightarrow\left(i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-m\right)\psi=\partial_{\mu}[0]=0,

(258)

one gets the Dirac equation for
\psi
field. The Hamiltonian density is given as

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\partial_{0}\psi+\partial_{0}\bar{\psi}\bar{\pi}-{\cal L}.

(259)

The conjugate momenta can be obtained as

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial(\partial_{0}\psi)}=i\psi^{\dagger},

(260)

\displaystyle{\bar{\pi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial(\partial_{0}{\bar{\psi}})}=0.

(261)

Using (
260
) and (
261
) in (
259
), the Hamiltonian density becomes

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\psi^{\dagger}\partial_{0}\psi-{\bar{\psi}}\left(i\partial\!\!\!/\penalty-m\right)\psi=i{\bar{\psi}}\gamma^{0}\partial_{0}\psi-i{\bar{\psi}}\gamma^{0}\partial_{0}\psi+i{\bar{\psi}}\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}\psi+{\bar{\psi}}m\psi

(262)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\bar{\psi}\left(\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}+m\right)\psi.

This is the Hamiltonian density for canonical ensemble.
However, allowing a local transformation,
i.e.
,
\alpha

depends on
X
, one gets

\displaystyle{\cal L}\rightarrow{\cal L}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}e^{i\alpha(X)}\left(i\partial\!\!\!/\penalty-m\right)\psi e^{-i\alpha(X)}=\bar{\psi}\left(i\partial\!\!\!/\penalty-m\right)\psi\,+\,\bar{\psi}\partial\!\!\!/\penalty\alpha(X)\psi

(263)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal L}\,+\,\bar{\psi}\partial\!\!\!/\penalty\alpha(X)\psi.

As seen if
\alpha(X)=\alpha
, is a constant, then
{\cal L}^{\prime}={\cal L}
, the Lagrangian density is invariant under global
symmetry. This symmetry will lead to a conserved current according to Noether’s theorem. By solving the equation of
motion for
\alpha
, one gets

\displaystyle{\partial_{\mu}}\left(\frac{\partial{\cal L}^{\prime}}{\partial(\partial^{\mu}\alpha)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal L}^{\prime}}{\partial\alpha}

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left[\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0,

(264)

where the conserved current is found as

\displaystyle j^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\,.

(265)

Now the temporal component
j^{0}=\bar{\psi}\gamma^{0}\psi=\bar{\psi}^{\dagger}\psi=\rho
is associated with conserved number density. The conserved
number can be obtained as

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}x\,j^{0}=\int d^{3}x\,\,\bar{\psi}^{\dagger}\psi=\int d^{3}x\,\,\rho\,.

(266)

Now, the new Hamiltonian density in presence of chemical potential associated with a conserved number becomes

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{d}-\mu\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\bar{\psi}\left(\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}+m\right)\psi-\mu\rho=\bar{\psi}\left(i\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}+m-\mu\gamma^{0}\right)\psi.

(267)

The corresponding new Lagrangian density becomes

\displaystyle{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}\left(i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-m+\mu\gamma^{0}\right)\psi,

(268)

which indicates that the presence of the chemical potential is like changing the zeroth component
of the gauge field (external field), through the substitution
\partial_{0}-i\mu
in the Lagrangian.

6.3.2
Partition function and pressure for free fermions

The partition function for free fermionic field reads from (
206
) as

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int\limits_{\psi(\bm{\vec{x}},0)=-\psi(\bm{\vec{x}},\beta)}{\cal D}[{\bar{\psi}}]\ {\cal D}[{\psi}]\ e^{\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}({t\rightarrow-i\tau})}.

(269)

As before, the Fourier transform of the fermionic fields
\psi(X)
and
\bar{\psi}(X)
can be written as

\displaystyle\psi(X)=\psi(\bm{\vec{x}},\tau)

\displaystyle{=\atop{t\rightarrow-i\tau}}\atop{k_{0}\rightarrow i\omega_{n}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{K}\,e^{-iK\cdot X}\,\psi(K)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\,e^{i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{-i\omega_{n}\tau}\,\psi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}(X)=\bar{\psi}(\bm{\vec{x}},\tau)

\displaystyle{=\atop{t\rightarrow-i\tau}}\atop{k_{0}\rightarrow i\omega_{n}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{K}\,e^{iK\cdot X}\,\bar{\psi}(K)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{V\beta}}\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\,e^{-i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{i\omega_{n}\tau}\,\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\,.

(270)

where
V
is the three volume. The Lagrangian density in (
268
) can now be written in Euclidean time as

\displaystyle{\cal L}({t\rightarrow-i\tau})

\displaystyle{=\atop{t\rightarrow-i\tau}}

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}\left(i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-m+\mu\gamma^{0}\right)\psi{=\atop{t\rightarrow-i\tau}}\bar{\psi}\left(i\gamma^{0}\partial_{0}-i\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}-m+\mu\gamma^{0}\right)\psi

(271)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\psi}\left(\gamma^{0}\partial_{\tau}+i\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}+m-\mu\gamma^{0}\right)\psi\,.

Using the Fourier transformed of fermionic fields in (
270
) we compute

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,{\cal L}({t\rightarrow-i\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{V\beta}\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\sum_{m,\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}}}e^{-i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{i\omega_{n}\tau}\,\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})

(272)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\left[\gamma^{0}\partial_{\tau}+i\gamma^{i}\partial_{i}+m-\mu\gamma^{0}\right]e^{i\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{-i\omega_{m}\tau}\,\psi(\omega_{m},\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{V\beta}\int\limits_{0}^{\beta}\,d\tau\int\,d^{3}{x}\,\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\sum_{m,\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}}}e^{-i\bm{\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{i\omega_{n}\tau}\,\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\left[\gamma^{0}(-i\omega_{m})+i\gamma^{i}(ik^{\prime}_{i})+m-\mu\gamma^{0}\right]e^{i\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{x}}}\,\,e^{-i\omega_{m}\tau}\,\psi(\omega_{m},\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{V\beta}\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\sum_{m,\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}}}\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\left[\gamma^{0}(-i\omega_{m})+i\gamma^{i}(ik^{\prime}_{i})+m-\mu\gamma^{0}\right]\psi(\omega_{m},\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\times\beta\delta(\omega_{n}-\omega_{m})\,V\delta^{3}(\bm{\vec{k}^{\prime}}-\bm{\vec{k}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\left[-\gamma^{0}(i\omega_{n}+\mu)-\gamma^{i}k_{i}+m\right]\psi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\left[S_{0}^{-1}((i\omega_{n}+\mu),\bm{\vec{k}})\right]\psi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}}),

where
S_{0}^{-1}(i\omega_{n}+\mu,\bm{\vec{k}})
is the inverse of free fermionic propagator in presence of chemical potential
\mu
.
The partition function in (
269
) becomes

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{n,\bm{\vec{k}}}\int\limits_{\tiny\mbox{\sf antiperiodic}}{\cal D}[{\bar{\psi}}]\ {\cal D}[{\psi}]\ e^{-\bar{\psi}(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})\left[S_{0}^{-1}((i\omega_{n}+\mu),\bm{\vec{k}})\right]\psi(\omega_{n},\bm{\vec{k}})}

(273)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{n,{k}}\mbox{det}\left[S_{0}^{-1}((i\omega_{n}+\mu),\bm{\vec{k}})\right],

where we have used the result of the functional integral in (
81
) involving Grassman variables.
The logarithm of
{\cal Z}_{0}
becomes

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n,k}\ln\,\mbox{det}\left[S_{0}^{-1}((i\omega_{n}+\mu),\bm{\vec{k}})\right]

(274)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n,k}\ln\mbox{det}\left[-\gamma^{0}(i\omega_{n}+\mu)-\gamma^{i}k_{i}+m\right]=\sum_{n,k}\ln\mbox{det}[Y].

Now one can write

\displaystyle\mbox{det}[Y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{det}\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}-(i\omega_{n}+\mu)&\hskip 28.90755pt0\\
0&(i\omega_{n}+\mu)\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&-\bm{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{k}}\\
\bm{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{k}}&\hskip 14.45377pt0\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}m&0\\
0&m\end{array}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{det}\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}-(i\omega_{n}+\mu)+m&\hskip 21.68121pt-\bm{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{k}}\\
\bm{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{k}}&(i\omega_{n}+\mu)+m\end{array}\right)\right\}\,,

where each element is a
2\times 2
matrix. Using the identity
(\bm{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{k}})^{2}=k^{2}
, one can compute the
determinant as

\displaystyle\mbox{det}[Y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[k^{2}+m^{2}-(i\omega_{n}+\mu)^{2}\right]^{2}.

(284)

Combining (
284
) and (
274
), one can write

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\sum_{n,{k}}\ln\left[k^{2}+m^{2}-(i\omega_{n}+\mu)^{2}\right]

(285)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2V\sum_{n}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\ln((\omega_{k}-\mu)-i\omega_{n})+\ln((\omega_{k}+\mu)+i\omega_{n})\right]\ ,

where
\omega_{k}=\sqrt{k^{2}+m^{2}}
and
\sum_{k}
is replaced by
V\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}
.

Now we will perform the frequency sum in (
285
):

First term: using (
144
), one can write

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\ln[(\omega_{k}-\mu)-i\omega_{n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T\sum_{n}\frac{1}{T}\ln[(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\ln[(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}]\,\frac{1}{2}\,\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{-1}{(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}}\right)\int dk_{0}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}}\right)\int dk_{0}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi iT}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}}\right)\frac{2}{\beta}\ln\left[\cosh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)\right]

(286)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint dk_{0}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}}\right)\ln\left[\cosh\left(\frac{\beta k_{0}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[\cosh\left(\frac{\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{{\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}/{2}}+e^{{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}/{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left[\frac{1}{2}e^{{\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}/{2}}\left(1-e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}{2}-\ln 2+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}{2}+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}\right)\right],

where again
\ln 2
is neglected as it is independent of temperature.

Second term:

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\ln[(\omega_{k}+\mu)+i\omega_{n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T\sum_{n}\frac{1}{T}\ln[(\omega_{k}-\mu)-k_{0}]=\ln\left[\cosh\left(\frac{\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}{2}\right)\right]

(287)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}{2}-\ln 2+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}{2}+\ln\left(1-e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}\right)\right].

Now using (
286
) and (
287
) in (
285
), one gets logarithm of the partition function

\displaystyle\ln{\cal Z}_{0}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2V\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\left[{\beta\omega_{k}}+\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}\right)+\ln\left(1+e^{+\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}\right)\right],

(288)

which agrees with that obtained in quantum statistical mechanics
in (
24
) and (
26
).

The free energy density for free fermionic field can be obtained as

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{T\ln{\cal Z}_{0}}{V}\,=-2T\,\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\left[{\beta\omega_{k}}+\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}\right)+\ln\left(1+e^{+\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}\right)\right].

(289)

In the infinite volume limit the pressure for fermionic field can be obtained as

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-F_{0}=2T\,\int\frac{d^{3}{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\left[{\beta\omega_{k}}+\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(\omega_{k}-\mu)}\right)+\ln\left(1+e^{+\beta(\omega_{k}+\mu)}\right)\right],

(290)

where the first term is the zero temperature part which should be dropped as it only shifts the vacuum energy.
After performing the integration of temperature dependent part, one obtains pressure for free massless fermion

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{7\pi^{2}T^{4}}{180}+\frac{\mu^{2}T^{2}}{6}+\frac{\mu^{4}}{12\pi^{2}}\,.

(291)

6.3.3
A reverse way: first number density and then pressure and entropy density for fermions

The partition function in Minkowski space time can be written

\displaystyle{\cal Z}(\beta,[\mu])=\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]{\cal D}[{\psi}]e^{i\int d^{4}X\mathcal{L}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};[\mu])}.

(292)

The pressure can be written as

{\cal P}(\beta;[\mu])=\frac{1}{\cal V}\ln{\cal Z}(T;[\mu])=\frac{T}{V}\ln{\cal Z}(T;[\mu])\ ,

(293)

where the four-volume,
{\cal V}=\beta V
with
V
is the three-volume.

Figure 22:
The Feynman diagram for number density originating from
\mu
derivative of the pressure diagram that brings
a
\gamma_{0}
.

Now the number density
\rho
can be obtained
HM
;
HMT
as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\rho

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal P}(\beta;[\mu])}{\partial\mu}=\frac{i}{{\cal V}{\cal Z}[\beta;j]}\ {\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]{\cal D}[\psi]\int d^{4}x\ {\bar{\psi}(x)}\gamma_{0}[\mu]\psi(x)}\,e^{\left({i\int d^{4}x{\cal L}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};[\mu])}\right)}\ .

(294)

The full fermionic propagator in presence of uniform
\mu
can be written as

\displaystyle i{\cal S}_{\alpha\sigma}[\mu](x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]{\cal D}[\psi]\psi_{\alpha}(x){\bar{\psi}_{\sigma}(x^{\prime})}\exp\left({i\int d^{4}x{\cal L}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};[\mu])}\right)}{\int{\cal D}[\bar{\psi}]{\cal D}[\psi]\exp\left({i\int d^{4}x{\cal L}(\psi,{\bar{\psi}};[\mu])}\right)}\ .

(295)

Now using (
295
) and performing the traces over
Dirac and coordinate indices in (
294
) one can write

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mbox{tr}\left[iS[\mu](K)\ (-i)\gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)\right]

(296)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mbox{tr}\left[S[\mu](K)\ \gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)\right]\,,

where ’tr’ indicates the trace over the Dirac indices.

Now if we consider free fermions, then the full propagator
S[\mu]
can be replaced by the free fermion
propagator
S_{0}[\mu]
and the number density in (
296
) can be written as

\displaystyle\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \,\int\!\frac{d^{4}K}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mbox{tr}\left[S_{0}[\mu](K)\ \gamma_{0}[\mu](K,-K;0)\right],

(297)

The expression for number density in (
297
) corresponds to
Feynman diagram in Fig.
22
.
The free fermionic propagator for momentum
K
in helicity representation is given in (
327
) as

S_{0}(K)=\frac{\gamma_{0}-\vec{\gamma}\cdot\hat{k}}{2d_{+}(k_{0},k)}\ +\frac{\gamma_{0}+\vec{\gamma}\cdot\hat{k}}{2d_{-}(k_{0},k)},

(298)

\mbox{with}\,\,\,d_{\pm}=k_{0}\mp k\ \ .

(299)

Using (
298
) in (
297
) and performing the trace over Dirac matrices,
we get

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(T,\mu)=2\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{k_{0}=(2n+1)\pi iT+\mu}\left[\frac{1}{k_{0}-k}+\frac{1}{k_{0}+k}\right].

(300)

We note here that the chemical potential is not considered in the propagator but
considered in the discrete frequency as
k_{0}=i\omega_{n}+\mu
. This shifts the pole of

\tanh
by an amount
\mu
in (
144
). This will lead to
same result as will see below.

For computing the frequency sum in (
300
), we use the standard technique of contour
integration as given in (
146
) in presence of
\mu
as

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\limits_{C}\left[\frac{1}{k_{0}-k}+\frac{1}{k_{0}+k}\right]\frac{\beta}{2}\mbox{tanh}\left(\frac{\beta(k_{0}-\mu)}{2}\right)dk_{0}=\frac{\beta}{2}\ \frac{1}{2\pi i}\times(-2\pi i)\sum{\mbox{Residues}}\ .

(301)

It is noted that the first term of (
301
) has a simple pole at

k_{0}=k
. On the other hand the second term also has a simple pole at
k_{0}=-k
. After calculating
the residues, one obtains the number density
HM
;
HMT

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(T,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\tanh\frac{\beta(k-\mu)}{2}-\tanh\frac{\beta(k+\mu)}{2}\right]

(302)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[n(k-\mu)-n(k+\mu)\right],

where
n(x)=1/(e^{\beta x}+1)
, is the Fermi-Dirac distribution function.

Now, by integrating the first line of (
302
) w.r.t.
\mu
, one obtains the pressure for non-interacting fermion gas as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{0}(T,\mu)=2T\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\beta k+\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(k-\mu)}\right)+\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(k+\mu)}\right)\right],

(303)

where the first term is the
zero-point energy that produces usual vacuum divergence. It also agrees with that obtained in (
290
).
The entropy density for non-interacting can be obtained from pressure as

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{0}(T,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\cal P}_{0}}{\partial T}=2\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big[\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(k-\mu)}\right)+\ln\left(1+e^{-\beta(k+\mu)}\right)

(304)

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{\beta(k-\mu)}{e^{\beta(k-\mu)}+1}+\frac{\beta(k+\mu)}{e^{\beta(k+\mu)}+1}\right].

7
General Structure of Fermionic Two-point Functions at
T\neq 0

7.1
Fermion Self-Energy

A theory possessing only fermions and gauge bosons with no bare masses for fermions is chirally invariant for all orders. It is to
be noted that the theory is also parity invariant. At
T=0
, chiral invariance has two implications: i) there are no
{\bar{\psi}}\psi
coupling in
any finite order of perturbation theory, ii) the general form of the fermion self-energy can be written as
HAW

\Sigma(P)=-{\cal A}P\!\!\!\!/\penalty,

(305)

for particle momentum
P\equiv(p_{0}=\omega,\bm{\vec{p}})
,
p=|\bm{\vec{p}}|
and
{\cal A}
is Lorentz invariant structure function which is function of
P^{2}
.

The effective fermion propagator can be written from (
316
) as

S^{\star}(P)=\frac{1}{P\!\!\!\!/\penalty-\Sigma(P)}=\frac{P\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{(1+{\cal A})P^{2}}.

(306)

The poles,
P^{2}=0
are on the light cone,
\omega=p
and
(1+{\cal A})
modifies the residues.

At
T\neq 0
, the above item i) still holds whereas item ii) does not. At
T\neq 0
, the system will not be in a vacuum because at such high temperature there will
be antiparticles present in equal numbers as the particles. This constitutes a heat bath which introduces a special Lorentz frame. So, the heat bath has
four velocity
u^{\mu}=(1,0,0,0)
with
u^{\mu}u_{\mu}=1
. The presence of four velocity means that the most general ansatz for fermion self-energy
HAW
will be of the form

\displaystyle\Sigma(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\cal A}P\!\!\!\!/\penalty-{\cal B}u\!\!\!/\penalty\,,

(307)

where
{\cal B}
is another Lorentz invariant structure function in addition to
{\cal A}
.
Since
P^{2}=\omega^{2}-p^{2}
, one can interpret
\omega=p_{0}=P^{\mu}u_{\mu}
and
p=\left(P^{\mu}u_{\mu}-P^{2}\right)^{1/2}
as Lorentz invariant energy and
momentum, respectively. The Lorentz invariant structure functions are obtained as follows:

We now write from (
307
) as

\displaystyle\Sigma P\!\!\!\!/\penalty

\displaystyle=-{\cal A}P^{2}-{\cal B}P\!\!\!\!/\penalty u\!\!\!/\penalty,

(308a)

\displaystyle\Sigma u\!\!\!/\penalty
